Horizon data: existence results and a near-horizon equation on general null hypersurfaces
Authors: Miguel Manzano, Marc Mars
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
In a spacetime \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {M},g)$$\end{document}(M,g), a horizon is a null hypersurface where the deformation tensor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}:=\pounds _{\eta }g$$\end{document}K:=£ηg of a null and tangent vector η η satisfies certain restrictions. In this work, we develop a formalism to study the geometry of general horizons (i.e. characterized by any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}$$\end{document}K), based on encoding the zeroth and first transverse derivatives of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}$$\end{document}K on null hypersurfaces detached from any ambient spacetime. We introduce the notions of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}$$\end{document}K-tuple and non-isolation tensor. The former encodes the order zero of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}$$\end{document}K, while the latter is a symmetric 2-covariant tensor that codifies the “degree of isolation" of a horizon. In particular, the non-isolation tensor vanishes for homothetic, Killing and isolated horizons. As an application we derive a generalized near-horizon equation, i.e., an identity that holds on any horizon (regardless of its topology or whether it contains fixed points), which relates the non-isolation tensor, a certain torsion one-form, and curvature terms. By restricting this equation to a cross-section one can recover the near-horizon equation of isolated horizons and the master equation of multiple Killing horizons. Our formalism allows us to prove two existence theorems for horizons. Specifically, we establish the necessary and sufficient conditions for a non-degenerate totally geodesic horizon with any prescribed non-isolation tensor to be embeddable in a spacetime satisfying any (non-necessarily Λ Λ-vacuum) field equations. We treat first the case of arbitrary topology, and then show how the result can be strengthened when the horizon admits a cross-section.

Introduction

For the purposes of this paper, horizons are null hypersurfaces endowed with a privileged null and tangent vector field η η which encodes certain symmetry property of the spacetime. They play a fundamental role in many gravitational contexts such as gravitational collapse, black hole uniqueness, singularity theorems, or null infinity, see e.g. [5, 8, 17, 20, 28, 47, 50, 51] and references therein. Prime examples of horizons are homothetic, conformal Killing and Killing horizons, but there are many other useful notions such as non-expanding, weakly isolated and isolated horizons [2–4, 18, 21, 23], or multiple Killing horizons [37]. Horizons usually mark the boundary of causally relevant regions of a spacetime, and their presence reveals interesting connections between its causal structure and the behaviour of symmetries, which often change causal character across the horizons. In particular, Killing horizons are linked to (local) isometries and hence to equilibrium states, whereas homothetic horizons arise for instance in self-similar evolution, most notably in critical gravitational collapse [19, 48]. In a more general context, homothetic horizons are also prominent objects in conformal geometry via the Fefferman-Graham ambient construction [15, 16].

The link between horizons and spacetime symmetries raises fundamental questions, such as what are the conditions for a spacetime to admit a horizon, or what data must be prescribed on a horizon so that it induces a specific type of symmetry. These issues have been widely studied in the literature, starting with the seminal work [45] where analytic, Ricci-flat (4-dimensional) spacetimes with a non-degenerate Killing horizon were shown to be uniquely determined by six functions on the horizon (a result that was later reformulated in more geometric terms, and extended to smooth spacetimes in [24]). For degenerate Killing horizons, several non-existence and uniqueness results have been proved, including non-existence of such horizons in static Λ Λ-vacuum spacetimes [6, 9, 52], uniqueness of Schwarzchild-de Sitter [22] and (local) uniqueness of the Kerr-horizon data in the (electro)vacuum rotating case [14, 25, 27, 29, 44]. In the case where the horizon is of bifurcate type, i.e. it consists of two transverse null hypersurfaces,the problem can be analyzed from a characteristic initial value perspective. Relevant results in this regard are the formulation of the characteristic problem on bifurcate Killing horizons [49] (see also [11]), the analyticity of the spacetime metric for bifurcate Killing horizons defined by a hypersurface-orthogonal Killing vector [7], and the fact that bifurcate Killing horizons with closed torsion one-form give rise to (and arise from) static Killing vectors [10]. Motivated by these works, the purpose of the present paper is to introduce a formalism for describing horizons as manifolds a priori detached from any ambient spacetime, and to exploit it to study the interplay between the existence of spacetimes with symmetries and the geometric properties of the horizons. The key point of the formalism is that it allows one to work with generic notions of horizons (i.e. far less restrictive than those of homothetic or a Killing horizons), as well as to deal with spacetimes satisfying any (i.e. non-necessarily Λ Λ-vacuum) field equations.

A particularly relevant object in this context is the so-called deformation tensor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}[\eta ]\mathop {=}\limits ^{{\textbf {def}}}\pounds _{\eta }g$$\end{document}K[η]=def£ηg of a spacetime \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {M},g)$$\end{document}(M,g), which accounts for the “degree of symmetry" of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {M},g)$$\end{document}(M,g) along η η. In fact, every notion of horizon imposes constraints on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}[\eta ]$$\end{document}K[η] and/or its derivatives. For instance, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}[\eta ]$$\end{document}K[η] and all its derivatives vanish on Killing horizons, and are proportional to derivatives of gg on homothetic horizons, whereas isolated horizons impose restrictions only up to the first transverse derivative of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}[\eta ]$$\end{document}K[η] [31]. We base our approach on capturing the geometric information of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}[\eta ]$$\end{document}K[η] (more specifically, its zeroth and first orders at the horizon) in terms of data—i.e. a set of tensor fields—on a detached null hypersurface. In this way, the formalism enables us to consider completely general horizons (i.e. with arbitrary \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}[\eta ]$$\end{document}K[η] and with no restrictions of the topology of the horizon), and then study specific types of horizon by particularizing the data set. Besides, we shall maintain full generality regarding the generator η η, which in particular is allowed to vanish on the horizon.

To capture the order zero of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}[\eta ]$$\end{document}K[η] on a detached null hypersurface \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}$$\end{document}H we introduce the concept of metric \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}$$\end{document}K-tuple (cf. Definitions 4.1 and 4.2), which involves two functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha ,\mathfrak {p}$$\end{document}α,p and a covector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\mathfrak {q}}$$\end{document}q, subject to certain restrictions. This notion is tailored so that, once \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}$$\end{document}H is embedded in a spacetime with embedding ϕ ϕ, the deformation tensor at the horizon is given by the quantities \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha {\textbf {U}} , \mathfrak {p},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak {q}}$$\end{document}αU,p,q, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {U}} $$\end{document}U is computable from the data and a posteriori agrees with the second fundamental form of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi (\mathcal {H})$$\end{document}ϕ(H). In particular, a Killing horizon of order zero is described at a non-embedded level by a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}$$\end{document}K-tuple with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\alpha {\textbf {U}} =0,\mathfrak {p}=0,\boldsymbol{\mathfrak {q}}=0\}$$\end{document}{αU=0,p=0,q=0}. A key result in this context is that any metric \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}$$\end{document}K-tuple can be embedded in a spacetime in such a way that the order zero of the deformation tensor is given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha {\textbf {U}} $$\end{document}αU, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\mathfrak {q}}$$\end{document}q and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {p}$$\end{document}p (Proposition 4.5). This is achieved by proving that the vector η η can always be extended off the horizon in such a way that its deformation tensor matches the prescribed data. The extension of η η is of course not unique, but its first order transverse derivative at the horizon is uniquely determined (cf. (4.6)).

The task of capturing the order one of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}[\eta ]$$\end{document}K[η] (i.e. its first transverse derivative) at a non-embedded level is more complicated, and involves commutators of Lie and covariant derivatives. For instance, isolated horizons [3] are totally geodesic null hypersurfaces for which the commutator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\pounds _{\eta },\overline{\nabla }]$$\end{document}[£η,∇¯] of the connection \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\nabla }$$\end{document}∇¯ induced from the spacetime and the Lie derivative along η η vanishes. For totally geodesic null hypersurfaces, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\nabla }$$\end{document}∇¯ is intrinsic to the horizon, hence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\pounds _{\eta },\overline{\nabla }]=0$$\end{document}[£η,∇¯]=0 is a property of the horizon and one can understand the tensor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\pounds _{\eta },\overline{\nabla }]$$\end{document}[£η,∇¯] as a measure of the “degree of non-isolation". For general null hypersurfaces, however, the induced connection \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\nabla }$$\end{document}∇¯ depends strongly on the choice of a rigging (i.e. a transverse vector along the hypersurface). Consequently, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\pounds _{\eta },\overline{\nabla }]$$\end{document}[£η,∇¯] is in general not suitable for characterizing horizon geometry, and one must find a tensor which encodes the same information as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\pounds _{\eta },\overline{\nabla }]$$\end{document}[£η,∇¯] in the totally geodesic case, but exhibits a better behavior under changes of rigging. In Section 4, we provide one such notion of non-isolation tensor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\Pi }^{\eta }$$\end{document}Πη (Definition 4.6), and show that in the embedded case it is related to the order one of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}[\eta ]$$\end{document}K[η] (Lemma 4.11). In particular, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\Pi }^{\eta }$$\end{document}Πη vanishes for isolated, homothetic and Killing horizons.

An interesting application of the results above is the so-called generalized near-horizon equation (Theorem 5.1). This is an identity that holds on any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}$$\end{document}K-tuple, which relates the non-isolation tensor, the surface gravity κ κ of η η, the extrinsic curvature of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi (\mathcal {H})$$\end{document}ϕ(H), a one-form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\omega }$$\end{document}ω, and the so-called constraint tensor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\mathcal {R}}$$\end{document}R [32, 38] (namely a tensor that encodes the tangential part of the ambient Ricci tensor at a non-embedded level). The one-form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\omega }$$\end{document}ω is an extension of the well-known torsion one-form in the sense that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\omega }$$\end{document}ω restricted to cross-sections (when they exist) coincides with it. Remarkably, the generalized near-horizon equation is valid for arbitrary topology (in particular, it does not require the existence of a cross-section), and for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}$$\end{document}K-tuple s possibly containing fixed points of η η. It also holds for arbitrary \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\mathcal {R}}$$\end{document}R, hence for any field equations one may wish to impose. More important is the fact that its restriction to cross-sections enables to recover both the near-horizon equation of isolated horizons [3, Eq. (5.3)] and the master equation of multiple Killing horizons [37, Eq. (60)] as particular cases. For the purposes of this work, one key aspect of the generalized near-horizon equation is that, when κ κ is no-where zero, it allows one to determine algebraically the extrinsic curvature of the horizon (i.e. first transverse derivatives of the spacetime metric in the embedded case) from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\omega }$$\end{document}ω, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\Pi }^{\eta }$$\end{document}Πη and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\mathcal {R}}$$\end{document}R. This result turns out to be essential to prove existence of a spacetime with a symmetry from initial data prescribed on a horizon (Section 6).

Two main results of the paper arise from applying the framework to study the problem of how to construct a spacetime from data on a totally geodesic horizon with nowhere zero κ κ. We obtain two existence results (Theorems 6.4 and 6.8). The first one establishes the necessary and sufficient conditions to be satisfied by the data to ensure existence of a spacetime \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {M},g)$$\end{document}(M,g) realizing the prescribed quantities. More precisely, the data consists of the order zero of the spacetime metric together with freely prescribed quantities α α, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\omega }$$\end{document}ω, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\Pi }^{\eta }$$\end{document}Πη and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\mathcal {R}}$$\end{document}R subject only to certain differential conditions, and the spacetime \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {M},g)$$\end{document}(M,g) is such that α α determines the vector field η η on the embedded horizon, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\omega }$$\end{document}ω is the generalized torsion one-form, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\Pi }^{\eta }$$\end{document}Πη is the non-isolation tensor of η η, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\mathcal {R}}$$\end{document}R coincides with the tangential components of the ambient Ricci tensor. The necessary and sufficient conditions, on the other hand, are a set of differential equations that, in particular, provide evolution equations for α α, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\omega }$$\end{document}ω and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\Pi }^{\eta }$$\end{document}Πη (or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\mathcal {R}}$$\end{document}R) along the null generators of the horizon (cf. (6.20)-(6.22)). These results therefore determine how to build spacetimes containing totally geodesic horizons with any “degree of non-isolation", any topology, possibly with fixed points, and such that the spacetime Ricci tensor satisfies any field equations. Since horizons admitting a cross-section S are of particular relevance in General Relativity, in Theorem 6.8 we provide the corresponding existence theorem with initial data on S. As a particular case, Theorems 6.4 and 6.8 can be applied to construct spacetimes with non-expanding, weakly isolated or Killing horizons. In fact, this is done in Remark 6.13 for horizons with vanishing second fundamental form and vanishing non-isolation tensor. The present paper, however, provides the necessary framework and tools to construct spacetimes with other, more evolutionary, types of horizons such as homothetic or conformal Killing horizons.

In the particular case when the non-isolation tensor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\Pi }^{\eta }$$\end{document}Πη vanishes identically and the constraint tensor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\mathcal {R}}$$\end{document}R is Λ Λ-vacuum, the necessary and sufficient conditions (6.20)-(6.22) simplify drastically. The third condition becomes trivial, while the first one just states that the surface gravity is constant. Thus, the only relevant condition that remains is (6.21). Up to a simple redefinition of variables, this is precisely the defining condition of the notion of abstract Killing horizon data (AKH) introduced in [42]. In [42] and its follow up paper [43], AKH data with constant non-zero surface gravity was shown to give rise to a spacetime \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {M},g)$$\end{document}(M,g) where the data can be embedded as a Killing horizon, and such that the Λ Λ-vacuum field equations are satisfied to infinite order on the horizon. Asymptotic uniqueness of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {M},g)$$\end{document}(M,g) at the horizon was also proven. Given that in the present paper we are not assuming any field equations, the existence theorem we establish is necessarily weaker concerning the properties of the spacetime one constructs (nothing can be said about higher order transverse derivatives at the horizon). However, it is stronger in the sense that it allows for general non-isolation tensor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\Pi }^{\eta }$$\end{document}Πη and constraint tensor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\mathcal {R}}$$\end{document}R. In combination with suitable field equations, our result can potentially give rise to existence and uniqueness theorems to all orders for spacetimes beyond Λ Λ-vacuum and/or admitting more general types of horizons.

The structure of the paper is as follows. In Section 2 we revisit the basic concepts and results of the formalism of hypersurface data [30, 32, 34, 35, 40], which is the mathematical framework used in the paper. Section 3 is devoted to studying commutators of Lie and covariant derivatives. In Section 4 we introduce the notions of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}$$\end{document}K-tuple and non-isolation tensor, which are then exploited in Section 5 to derive the generalized near-horizon equation. Finally, in Section 6 we demonstrate the aforementioned existence results in the case of totally geodesic initial data.

Notation and conventions

In this paper, all manifolds are smooth, connected and without boundary. Given a manifold \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {M}$$\end{document}M we use \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}\left( \mathcal {M}\right) \mathop {=}\limits ^{{\textbf {def}}}C^{\infty }\left( \mathcal {M},\mathbb {R}\right) $$\end{document}FM=defC∞M,R, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}^{\star }\left( \mathcal {M}\right) \subset \mathcal {F}\left( \mathcal {M}\right) $$\end{document}F⋆M⊂FM for the subset of no-where zero functions. The tangent bundle is denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T\mathcal {M}$$\end{document}TM, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma \left( T\mathcal {M}\right) $$\end{document}ΓTM is the set of sections (i.e. vector fields). We use \pounds £, d for the Lie derivative and exterior derivative. Both tensorial and abstract index notation will be employed. We work in arbitrary dimension \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {n}\ge 2$$\end{document}n≥2 and use the following sets of indices:1.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \alpha ,\beta ,...=0,1,2,...,\mathfrak {n};\qquad a,b,...=1,2,...,\mathfrak {n};\qquad A,B,...=2,...,\mathfrak {n}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}α,β,...=0,1,2,...,n;a,b,...=1,2,...,n;A,B,...=2,...,n.When index-free notation is used (and only then) we shall distinguish covariant tensors with boldface. As usual, parenthesis (resp. brackets) denote symmetrization (resp. antisymmetrization) of indices. The symmetrized tensor product is defined by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A\otimes _s B\equiv \frac{1}{2}(A\otimes B+B\otimes A)$$\end{document}A⊗sB≡12(A⊗B+B⊗A). We write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {tr}_B\boldsymbol{A}$$\end{document}trBA for the trace of a symmetric (0, 2)-tensor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{A}$$\end{document}A with respect to a (2, 0)-tensor B. Our notation and convention for the curvature operator of a connection D is1.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} R^{D}(X,W) Z \mathop {=}\limits ^{{\textbf {def}}}\left( D_X D_W - D_W D_X - D_{[X,W]} \right) Z, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}RD(X,W)Z=defDXDW-DWDX-D[X,W]Z,and we write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {Hess }}^D$$\end{document}HessD for the Hessian operator of D. In any Lorentzian manifold \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {M},g)$$\end{document}(M,g), the scalar product of two vectors is written as g(X, Y), and we use \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g^{\sharp }$$\end{document}g♯ and ∇ ∇ for the inverse and the Levi-Civita derivative of g respectively. Our signature for Lorentzian manifolds \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left( \mathcal {M},g\right) $$\end{document}M,g is (-,+,...,+)(-,+,...,+).

Throughout the paper, the term horizon will refer to an embedded null hypersurface admitting a privileged vector field that is null and tangent everywhere along the hypersurface. Based on this, one can construct increasingly restrictive notions of horizon by imposing conditions on the deformation tensor of the privileged vector field. Specifically, the following types of horizons shall play a role later on: totally geodesic null hypersurfaces (Sections 3, 5 and 6), homothetic horizons (Section 4), isolated horizons (Section 5), and multiple Killing horizons (Section 5). The precise definitions will be given as needed.

Preliminaries: The formalism of hypersurface data

In this section, we present the basic notions of the hypersurface data formalism, and collect a number of results for later use. Since this work deals with null hypersurfaces, we only present the formalism in the null case. For further details we refer to [34, 35] (see also [30, 32, 40]).

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}$$\end{document}H be a smooth \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {n}$$\end{document}n-manifold endowed with a symmetric (0, 2)-tensor field γ γ of signature (0,+,...,+)(0,+,...,+), a covector field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\ell }$$\end{document}ℓ and a scalar function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell ^{(2)}$$\end{document}ℓ(2). The tuple \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\mathcal {H},\gamma ,\boldsymbol{\ell },\ell ^{(2)}\}$$\end{document}{H,γ,ℓ,ℓ(2)} defines null metric hypersurface data (or null metric data for short) provided that the square \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathfrak {n}+1)$$\end{document}(n+1)-matrix2.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \boldsymbol{\mathcal {A}}\mathop {=}\limits ^{{\textbf {def}}}\left( \hspace{-0.1cm} \begin{array}{cc} \gamma _{ab} & \ell _a\\ \ell _b & \ell ^{(2)}\end{array} \hspace{-0.1cm}\right) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}A=defγabℓaℓbℓ(2)is non-degenerate everywhere on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}$$\end{document}H. A null metric data \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\mathcal {H},\gamma ,\boldsymbol{\ell },\ell ^{(2)}\}$$\end{document}{H,γ,ℓ,ℓ(2)} equipped with an additional symmetric (0, 2)-tensor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {Y}} $$\end{document}Y defines null hypersurface data \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{ \mathcal {H},\gamma ,\boldsymbol{\ell }, \ell ^{(2)}, {\textbf {Y}} \}$$\end{document}{H,γ,ℓ,ℓ(2),Y}.

Given null metric data, one can uniquely define a symmetric (2, 0)-tensor field P and a vector field n as the entries of the inverse of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\mathcal {A}}$$\end{document}A [34]. Remark 2.1

We have fixed the signature of γ γ to be (0,+,… ,+)(0,+,⋯,+) for concreteness. All results below apply when γ γ has signature \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(0,-,\dots ,-,+,\dots ,+)$$\end{document}(0,-,⋯,-,+,⋯,+). The only difference is that the ambient spaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {M},g)$$\end{document}(M,g) are no longer Lorentzian but semi-Riemannian (with same signature as the tensor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\mathcal {A}}$$\end{document}A in (2.1)).

The Lie derivative of a connection

Given a manifold endowed with a vector field Z and a connection D, one can define the tensor “Lie derivative of D along Z", which we denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {\Sigma }[Z]\mathop {=}\limits ^{{\textbf {def}}}\pounds _{Z}D$$\end{document}Σ[Z]=def£ZD. Tensor fields \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {\Sigma }[Z]$$\end{document}Σ[Z] have been widely studied (e.g. in [53], to which we refer for further details), albeit they are not so commonly used in the literature on hypersurface geometry. They, however, play a central role when the vector field Z is privileged in some sense (e.g. when it defines a horizon), as they encode geometric properties of Z and, at the same time, properties of the curvature tensor of D (see e.g. equation (3.4) below).

In the works [31, 41–43], various kinds of tensors “Lie derivative of a connection" have been defined and analyzed, mostly for connections defined on the ambient space. In this paper, however, we take a slightly different approach and focus on tensors \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {\Sigma }[Z]$$\end{document}Σ[Z] defined at the abstract level, i.e. for non-embedded hypersurface data. More specifically, we will introduce the tensor fields \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathop {\Sigma }\limits ^{\circ }}\mathop {=}\limits ^{{\textbf {def}}}\pounds _n{\mathop {\nabla }\limits ^{\circ }}$$\end{document}Σ∘=def£n∇∘ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\Sigma }[\overline{\eta }]\mathop {=}\limits ^{{\textbf {def}}}\pounds _{\overline{\eta }}\overline{\nabla }$$\end{document}Σ¯[η¯]=def£η¯∇¯, where as before \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\eta }$$\end{document}η¯ is a gauge-invariant vector field proportional to n. We will obtain their explicit expressions and analyze some of their properties. As we shall see, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\Sigma }[\overline{\eta }]$$\end{document}Σ¯[η¯] plays an essential role in the study of abstract horizons. For instance, it is key for the derivation of the so-called generalized near-horizon equation (see Section 5), an identity that generalizes the well-known near-horizon equation of isolated horizons [2–4, 18, 21, 23] as well as the so-called master equation of multiple Killing horizons [37], to generic null hypersurfaces with any topology and with a possibly non-empty set of zeroes of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\eta }$$\end{document}η¯.

Consider a smooth manifold \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}$$\end{document}V endowed with an affine connection D and a vector field Z. The 1-contravariant, 2-covariant tensor field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {\Sigma }[Z]\mathop {=}\limits ^{{\textbf {def}}}\pounds _{Z}D$$\end{document}Σ[Z]=def£ZD is defined by [53]3.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {\Sigma }[Z](X,W) \mathop {=}\limits ^{{\textbf {def}}}\pounds _{Z}D_XW-D_X\pounds _{Z}W-D_{\pounds _ZX}W,\qquad \forall X,W\in \Gamma (T\mathcal {V}). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Σ[Z](X,W)=def£ZDXW-DX£ZW-D£ZXW,∀X,W∈Γ(TV).This tensor is symmetric when D is torsion-free, which we shall assume from now on. Lemma 3.1

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}$$\end{document}V be a manifold, Z a vector field, D a torsion-free connection, and α α a scalar function. For any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X,W\in \Gamma (T\mathcal {V})$$\end{document}X,W∈Γ(TV), the vector fields \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {\Sigma }[\alpha Z](X,W)$$\end{document}Σ[αZ](X,W) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {\Sigma }[Z](X,W)$$\end{document}Σ[Z](X,W) are related by3.6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {\Sigma }[\alpha Z](X,W)= \alpha \mathbb {\Sigma }[Z](X,W)+ {\textbf {Hess}} ^D(\alpha )(X,W) Z +X(\alpha )D_WZ +W(\alpha )D_X Z. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Σ[αZ](X,W)=αΣ[Z](X,W)+HessD(α)(X,W)Z+X(α)DWZ+W(α)DXZ.

Proof

In the computation, we shall use the identity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pounds _{\alpha Z}W=\alpha \pounds _{Z}W-W(\alpha )Z$$\end{document}£αZW=α£ZW-W(α)Z several times. From the definition (3.1), it follows\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {\Sigma }[\alpha Z](X,W) =&\alpha \pounds _{Z}D_XW-(D_XW)(\alpha )Z - D_X\big ( \alpha \pounds _{Z}W-W(\alpha )Z\big ) \\&\quad -D_{\alpha \pounds _{Z}X-X(\alpha )Z}W\\ =&\alpha \pounds _{Z}D_XW -(D_XW)(\alpha )Z -X(\alpha )\pounds _{Z}W -\alpha D_X\pounds _{Z}W\\&+X\big (W(\alpha )\big )Z +W(\alpha )D_X Z -\alpha D_{\pounds _{Z}X}W +X(\alpha )D_{Z}W, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Σ[αZ](X,W)=α£ZDXW-(DXW)(α)Z-DX(α£ZW-W(α)Z)-Dα£ZX-X(α)ZW=α£ZDXW-(DXW)(α)Z-X(α)£ZW-αDX£ZW+X(W(α))Z+W(α)DXZ-αD£ZXW+X(α)DZW,which upon using (3.1), the fact that D is torsion-free (so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-\pounds _Z W + D_Z W = D_W Z$$\end{document}-£ZW+DZW=DWZ) and the definition of the hessian operator of D, yields (3.6). □ □

Now, any null metric data \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\mathcal {H},\gamma ,\boldsymbol{\ell },\ell ^{(2)}\}$$\end{document}{H,γ,ℓ,ℓ(2)} gives rise to a vector field n that is privileged in the sense that its direction (but not its scale) remains unchanged by gauge transformations (cf. (2.25)). It therefore makes sense to introduce the tensor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathop {\Sigma }\limits ^{\circ }}\mathop {=}\limits ^{{\textbf {def}}}\pounds _n{\mathop {\nabla }\limits ^{\circ }}$$\end{document}Σ∘=def£n∇∘, which will in fact be useful later for the calculation of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\Sigma }[\overline{\eta }]$$\end{document}Σ¯[η¯]. Observe that we do not reflect the n-dependence in the notation for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathop {\Sigma }\limits ^{\circ }}$$\end{document}Σ∘. The explicit expression of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathop {\Sigma }\limits ^{\circ }}$$\end{document}Σ∘ in terms of metric data is obtained next.

As mentioned before, the case when a null hypersurface data \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{ \mathcal {H},\gamma ,\boldsymbol{\ell }, \ell ^{(2)}, {\textbf {Y}} \}$$\end{document}{H,γ,ℓ,ℓ(2),Y} admits a gauge-invariant vector field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\eta }=\alpha n$$\end{document}η¯=αn with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in \mathcal {F}(\mathcal {H})$$\end{document}α∈F(H) is particularly relevant, as it allows one to analyze the geometry of horizons. In these circumstances, the vector field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\eta }$$\end{document}η¯ is privileged in the sense that it is a property of the abstract null hypersurface \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}$$\end{document}H. Therefore, it is to be expected that a number of fundamental geometric properties of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}$$\end{document}H are encoded in tensor fields of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pounds _{\overline{\eta }}D$$\end{document}£η¯D where D is an abstractly defined connection. The fact that many horizons are totally geodesic null hypersurfaces (hence characterized by null data with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {U}} =0$$\end{document}U=0), together with the relation (2.18) between the hypersurface connection \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\nabla }$$\end{document}∇¯ and the Levi-Civita derivative of g in the embedded case, strongly suggests that the appropriate object for addressing horizon geometry is the tensor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\Sigma }[\overline{\eta }]\mathop {=}\limits ^{{\textbf {def}}}\pounds _{\overline{\eta }}\overline{\nabla }$$\end{document}Σ¯[η¯]=def£η¯∇¯. Accordingly, we devote the remainder of this section to deriving its explicit expression, and obtaining its gauge behaviour. The analysis of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\Sigma }[\overline{\eta }]$$\end{document}Σ¯[η¯] will allow us to define a tensor field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\Pi }^{\overline{\eta }}$$\end{document}Πη¯ (see Section 4) whose properties are useful to characterize horizons at an abstract level. We start by deriving the explicit form of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\Sigma }[\overline{\eta }]$$\end{document}Σ¯[η¯].

Lemma 3.4

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{ \mathcal {H},\gamma ,\boldsymbol{\ell }, \ell ^{(2)}, {\textbf {Y}} \}$$\end{document}{H,γ,ℓ,ℓ(2),Y} be null hypersurface data and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\eta }=\alpha n$$\end{document}η¯=αn, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in \mathcal {F}(\mathcal {H})$$\end{document}α∈F(H) a gauge-invariant vector field. Then, 9113.17\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {G}_{(z,V)}\left( \overline{\Sigma }[\overline{\eta }]\right) =&\overline{\Sigma }[\overline{\eta }]+\Big ( \alpha \pounds _{n}V-V(\alpha )n\Big )\otimes {\textbf {U}} +\alpha V\otimes \pounds _{n}{\textbf {U}} ,\end{aligned}$$\end{document}G(z,V)Σ¯[η¯]=Σ¯[η¯]+(α£nV-V(α)n)⊗U+αV⊗£nU,3.18\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \nonumber \mathcal {G}_{(z,V)}\left( \boldsymbol{\ell }\left( \overline{\Sigma }[\overline{\eta }]\right) \right) =&z\Big ( \boldsymbol{\ell }\big ( \overline{\Sigma }[\overline{\eta }]\big )+ \gamma (V,\overline{\Sigma }[\overline{\eta }])\Big )+z\Big (\alpha \boldsymbol{\ell }\left( \pounds _nV\right) -V(\alpha )+\alpha \gamma (V,\pounds _nV)\Big ){\textbf {U}} \nonumber \\&+\alpha z\Big (\boldsymbol{\ell }(V)+\gamma (V,V)\Big )\pounds _{n}{\textbf {U}} . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}G(z,V)ℓΣ¯[η¯]=z(ℓ(Σ¯[η¯])+γ(V,Σ¯[η¯]))+z(αℓ£nV-V(α)+αγ(V,£nV))U+αz(ℓ(V)+γ(V,V))£nU.

Proof

Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\eta }$$\end{document}η¯ is gauge-invariant, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {G}_{(z,V)}\big (\overline{\Sigma }[\overline{\eta }]\big )=\mathcal {G}_{(z,V)}\big (\pounds _{\overline{\eta }}\overline{\nabla }\big )=\pounds _{\overline{\eta }}\big (\mathcal {G}_{(z,V)}\overline{\nabla }\big ){\mathop {=}\limits ^{\text {(2.30)}}}\pounds _{\overline{\eta }}\big (\overline{\nabla }+V\otimes {\textbf {U}} \big )=\overline{\Sigma }[\overline{\eta }]+\pounds _{\overline{\eta }}V\otimes {\textbf {U}} +V\otimes \pounds _{\overline{\eta }}{\textbf {U}} $$\end{document}G(z,V)(Σ¯[η¯])=G(z,V)(£η¯∇¯)=£η¯(G(z,V)∇¯)=(2.30)£η¯(∇¯+V⊗U)=Σ¯[η¯]+£η¯V⊗U+V⊗£η¯U, from where (3.17) follows after using \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pounds _{\overline{\eta }}{\textbf {U}} =\pounds _{\alpha n}{\textbf {U}} {\mathop {=}\limits ^{\text {(2.9)}}}\alpha \pounds _n{\textbf {U}} $$\end{document}£η¯U=£αnU=(2.9)α£nU. Equation (3.18) is a consequence of the gauge transformation of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\ell }$$\end{document}ℓ (cf. (2.23)). □ □

For totally geodesic null hypersurfaces (i.e. when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {U}} =0$$\end{document}U=0), the tensor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\Sigma }[\overline{\eta }]$$\end{document}Σ¯[η¯] is gauge-invariant while its contraction with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\ell }$$\end{document}ℓ exhibits the remarkably simple gauge-behaviour \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {G}_{(z,V)}\left( \boldsymbol{\ell }\left( \overline{\Sigma }[\overline{\eta }]\right) \right) = z\boldsymbol{\ell }\big ( \overline{\Sigma }[\overline{\eta }]\big )$$\end{document}G(z,V)ℓΣ¯[η¯]=zℓ(Σ¯[η¯]) (because \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma (\overline{\Sigma }[\overline{\eta }],\cdot )=0$$\end{document}γ(Σ¯[η¯],·)=0, by (3.16)). The restriction \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {U}} =0$$\end{document}U=0, however, is quite limiting and excludes physically and mathematically relevant classes of horizons, for instance homothetic or conformal Killing horizons. In that case, the gauge properties of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\Sigma }[\overline{\eta }]$$\end{document}Σ¯[η¯] become considerably more involved, which makes this tensor less suitable as a characterizing object. This motivates the introduction of the so-called non-isolation tensor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\Pi }^{\overline{\eta }}$$\end{document}Πη¯, a tensor field closely related to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\Sigma }[\overline{\eta }]$$\end{document}Σ¯[η¯] but with better gauge-behaviour. This is done in the next section.

Deformation tensor of an ambient vector: the notion of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}$$\end{document}K-tuple

Following common terminology in the literature, we shall use the term “horizon” in the minimal sense of a null hypersurface \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi (\mathcal {H})$$\end{document}ϕ(H) embedded in a spacetime \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {M},g)$$\end{document}(M,g) admitting a privileged vector field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta \in \Gamma (T\mathcal {M})$$\end{document}η∈Γ(TM) that becomes null and tangent to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi (\mathcal {H})\subset \mathcal {M}$$\end{document}ϕ(H)⊂M. In this setting, the so-called deformation tensor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}[\eta ]\mathop {=}\limits ^{{\textbf {def}}}\pounds _{\eta }g$$\end{document}K[η]=def£ηg plays a fundamental role; in particular \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}[\eta ]\vert _{\phi (\mathcal {H})}$$\end{document}K[η]|ϕ(H) is proportional to g for homothetic or conformal Killing horizons, and vanishes for Killing horizons. Given the importance of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}[\eta ]$$\end{document}K[η], it is natural to seek a purely abstract notion of horizon data that captures its value at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi (\mathcal {H})$$\end{document}ϕ(H). This leads us to the concept of (metric) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}$$\end{document}K-tuple, which we introduce next.

Definition 4.1

A set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\mathcal {H},\gamma ,\boldsymbol{\ell },\ell ^{(2)}\}$$\end{document}{H,γ,ℓ,ℓ(2)} is null metric data, and (ii) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha ,\mathfrak {p}\in \mathcal {F}(\mathcal {H})$$\end{document}α,p∈F(H), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} Remark 4.3

In the embedded case the quantities \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha {\textbf {U}} $$\end{document}αU, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\mathfrak {q}}$$\end{document}q and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {p}$$\end{document}p encode all the components of the deformation tensor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}[\eta ]$$\end{document}K[η] at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi (\mathcal {H})$$\end{document}ϕ(H). Note also that the function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {w}$$\end{document}w, defined in (4.2), satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi ^{\star }\big (\mathcal {K}[\eta ](\xi ,\phi _{\star }n)\big ) = \mathfrak {w}$$\end{document}ϕ⋆(K[η](ξ,ϕ⋆n))=w. We emphasize that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha {\textbf {U}} $$\end{document}αU, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\mathfrak {q}}$$\end{document}q and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {p}$$\end{document}p are fully arbitrary.

Naturally, two questions arise at this point. The first one is whether the gauge-behaviours (4.1) are consistent with changes in the rigging in the embedded case. The second is under which conditions an embedded null metric data can be completed with a tuple \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\alpha ,\mathfrak {p},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak {q}}\}$$\end{document}{α,p,q} satisfying (4.1) to obtain an embedded metric \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}$$\end{document}K-tuple. These issues are addressed in the following remark and proposition.
