For the necessity, assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\mathcal {H},\gamma ,\boldsymbol{\ell },\ell ^{(2)},\alpha ,\mathfrak {p},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak {q}}\}$$\end{document}{H,γ,ℓ,ℓ(2),α,p,q} is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\phi ,\xi )$$\end{document}(ϕ,ξ)-embedded in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {M},g)$$\end{document}(M,g). Then, by Definition 4.2 there exists an extension η η of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi _{\star }\overline{\eta }$$\end{document}ϕ⋆η¯ so that (4.4) holds. Such extension necessarily verifies (4.6) by [31, Thm. 4.1]. □ □

As mentioned before, the tensor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\Sigma }[\overline{\eta }]$$\end{document}Σ¯[η¯] is not ideal for characterizing the horizon geometry due to its gauge transformation properties. To describe horizon geometry in more favorable terms, we introduce a new tensor field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\Pi }^{\overline{\eta }}$$\end{document}Πη¯, called the non-isolation tensor. We first present its abstract definition together with an alternative expression for it. Then we obtain its gauge transformation, and explore its geometric meaning by considering the embedded case.

Definition 4.6

Consider a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}$$\end{document}K-tuple \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\mathcal {H},\gamma ,\boldsymbol{\ell },\ell ^{(2)},{\textbf {Y}} ,\alpha ,\mathfrak {p},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak {q}}\}$$\end{document}{H,γ,ℓ,ℓ(2),Y,α,p,q} with the corresponding gauge-invariant vector field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\eta }\mathop {=}\limits ^{{\textbf {def}}}\alpha n$$\end{document}η¯=defαn. The non-isolation tensor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\Pi }^{\overline{\eta }}$$\end{document}Πη¯ is the symmetric (0, 2)-tensor field defined by4.7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \boldsymbol{\Pi }^{\overline{\eta }}\mathop {=}\limits ^{{\textbf {def}}}\boldsymbol{\ell }\left( \overline{\Sigma }[\overline{\eta }]\right) -\frac{1}{2}\left( \alpha n\big (\ell ^{(2)}\big )-\mathfrak {p}\right) {\textbf {U}} -\alpha \ell ^{(2)}\pounds _n{\textbf {U}} . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Πη¯=defℓΣ¯[η¯]-12αn(ℓ(2))-pU-αℓ(2)£nU.

Remark 4.7

By inserting (3.15) into (4.7), one obtains the following alternative expression for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\Pi }^{\overline{\eta }}$$\end{document}Πη¯:4.8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Pi ^{\overline{\eta }}_{ab}&= {\mathop {\nabla }\limits ^{\circ }}_a{\mathop {\nabla }\limits ^{\circ }}_b\alpha +2({\mathop {\nabla }\limits ^{\circ }}_{(a}\alpha )\omega _{b)}+2\alpha {\mathop {\nabla }\limits ^{\circ }}_{(a}s_{b)}+n(\alpha )Y _{ab}-\alpha (\pounds _{n}{\textbf {Y}} )_{ab}\nonumber \\&\quad +\frac{1}{2}\left( \alpha n(\ell ^{(2)})+\mathfrak {p}\right) U _{ab}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Πabη¯=∇∘a∇∘bα+2(∇∘(aα)ωb)+2α∇∘(asb)+n(α)Yab-α(£nY)ab+12αn(ℓ(2))+pUab.

Remark 4.8

The name non-isolation tensor is justified by the fact that, when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {U}} =0$$\end{document}U=0 (i.e. when the abstract hypersurface has vanishing second fundamental form), it holdsIn this case, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\Pi }^{\overline{\eta }}=0$$\end{document}Πη¯=0 is therefore equivalent to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\Sigma }[\overline{\eta }]\mathop {=}\limits ^{{\textbf {def}}}\pounds _{\overline{\eta }}\overline{\nabla }=0$$\end{document}Σ¯[η¯]=def£η¯∇¯=0, which is the defining condition of an isolated horizon [3]. Thus, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\Pi }^{\overline{\eta }}$$\end{document}Πη¯ accounts for the “level of non-isolation" of a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}$$\end{document}K-tuple.

Lemma 4.9

Consider a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}$$\end{document}K-tuple \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\mathcal {H},\gamma ,\boldsymbol{\ell },\ell ^{(2)},{\textbf {Y}} ,\alpha ,\mathfrak {p},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak {q}}\}$$\end{document}{H,γ,ℓ,ℓ(2),Y,α,p,q} and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\eta }\mathop {=}\limits ^{{\textbf {def}}}\alpha n$$\end{document}η¯=defαn be its associated gauge-invariant vector field. A notion of “non-isolation tensor " was presented in [33, Def. 1] for an embedded null hypersurface \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {H}$$\end{document}H with vanishing second fundamental form. While in [33] this object was not directly related to the Lie derivative of the spacetime Levi-Civita connection ∇ ∇, by comparison with (3.4) it follows that it actually coincides with the tensor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pounds _{\nu }\nabla $$\end{document}£ν∇ restricted to directions tangent to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {H}$$\end{document}H. The notion of non-isolation tensor in [33], however, is not abstract, applies only in the case when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {U}} =0$$\end{document}U=0, and involves a null vector tangent to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {H}$$\end{document}H which is not allowed to vanish therein. In the more recent work [31, Eq. (5.6)], we generalized the non-isolation tensor of [33] to null hypersurfaces with arbitrary tensor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {U}} $$\end{document}U embedded in a spacetime \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {M},g)$$\end{document}(M,g) admitting a privileged vector field η η that is null and tangent along the hypersurface, and which is allowed to vanish. Nevertheless, the definition of [31] was still non-abstract, as it arose from computing certain components of the tensor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pounds _{\eta }\nabla $$\end{document}£η∇, and it involved the pull-back \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi ^{\star }(\pounds _{\xi }\mathcal {K}[\eta ])$$\end{document}ϕ⋆(£ξK[η]). In the present paper we have developed a framework, based on the notion of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}$$\end{document}K-tuple, that enables us to define and study \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\Pi }^{\overline{\eta }}$$\end{document}Πη¯ at a purely abstract level, and endow it with a clear geometric meaning that relates it to the tensor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pounds _{\eta }\nabla $$\end{document}£η∇ in the embedded case, as we shall see next.

Consider a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}$$\end{document}K-tuple \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\mathcal {H},\gamma ,\boldsymbol{\ell },\ell ^{(2)},{\textbf {Y}} ,\alpha ,\mathfrak {p},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak {q}}\}$$\end{document}{H,γ,ℓ,ℓ(2),Y,α,p,q} that is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\phi ,\xi )$$\end{document}(ϕ,ξ)-embedded in a Lorentzian manifold \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {M},g)$$\end{document}(M,g). Let η η be an extension of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi _{\star }\overline{\eta }$$\end{document}ϕ⋆η¯ off \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi (\mathcal {H})$$\end{document}ϕ(H) such that (4.4) holds. We want to relate the non-isolation tensor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\Pi }^{\overline{\eta }}$$\end{document}Πη¯ with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}[\eta ]$$\end{document}K[η] via the tensor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma [\eta ]\mathop {=}\limits ^{{\textbf {def}}}\pounds _{\eta }\nabla $$\end{document}Σ[η]=def£η∇. Particularizing (3.3) for Z=η Z=η, D=∇ D=∇, T=gT=g and multiplying by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g^{\mu \lambda }$$\end{document}gμλ, one finds [31]4.10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Sigma [\eta ]^{\lambda }{} _{\alpha \beta } =\frac{1}{2}g^{\mu \lambda }\big ( \nabla _{\alpha } \mathcal {K}[\eta ]_{\beta \mu }+\nabla _{\beta } \mathcal {K}[\eta ]_{\alpha \mu }-\nabla _{\mu } \mathcal {K}[\eta ]_{\alpha \beta }\big ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Σ[η]λαβ=12gμλ(∇αK[η]βμ+∇βK[η]αμ-∇μK[η]αβ).The tensor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma [\eta ]$$\end{document}Σ[η] is independent of the choice of rigging, hence its value at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi (\mathcal {H})$$\end{document}ϕ(H) must be gauge-invariant. The expression of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma [\eta ]$$\end{document}Σ[η] at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi (\mathcal {H})$$\end{document}ϕ(H) was obtained in [31, We conclude the section by providing the relation between the non-isolation tensor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\Pi }^{\overline{\eta }}$$\end{document}Πη¯ and the first transverse derivative of the deformation tensor for a given embedded \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}$$\end{document}K-tuple.

Lemma 4.11

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\mathcal {H},\gamma ,\boldsymbol{\ell },\ell ^{(2)},{\textbf {Y}} ,\alpha ,\mathfrak {p},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak {q}}\}$$\end{document}{H,γ,ℓ,ℓ(2),Y,α,p,q} be a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}$$\end{document}K-tuple \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\phi ,\xi )$$\end{document}(ϕ,ξ)-embedded in a Lorentzian manifold \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {M},g)$$\end{document}(M,g), and η η be the corresponding extension of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi _{\star }\overline{\eta }\mathop {=}\limits ^{{\textbf {def}}}\phi _{\star }(\alpha n)$$\end{document}ϕ⋆η¯=defϕ⋆(αn) off \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi ( \mathcal {H})$$\end{document}ϕ(H). A near-horizon equation on general null hypersurfaces

Given a Lorentzian manifold \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {M},g)$$\end{document}(M,g), an embedded null hypersurface \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {H}$$\end{document}H is said to be totally geodesic if its second fundamental form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {K }} ^{\nu }(X,W) \mathop {=}\limits ^{{\textbf {def}}}g(\nabla _X \nu , W), \, X,W \in \Gamma (T\mathscr {H})$$\end{document}Kν(X,W)=defg(∇Xν,W),X,W∈Γ(TH), vanishes on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {H}$$\end{document}H. Common examples of totally geodesic null hypersurfaces are non-expanding, weakly isolated and isolated horizons [2–4, 18, 21, 23], or Killing horizons [17, 51]. When \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {K }} ^{\nu }=0$$\end{document}Kν=0, any null (not necessarily everywhere non-zero) vector field η η tangent to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {H}$$\end{document}H is proportional to ν ν, and therefore also verifies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g(\nabla _X\eta ,W)=0$$\end{document}g(∇Xη,W)=0. Thus, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nabla _{X} \eta \vert _{\widetilde{\mathcal {H}}}$$\end{document}∇Xη|H~ is null and tangent to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{\mathcal {H}}$$\end{document}H~. Denoting by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}$$\end{document}S the set of points of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{\mathcal {H}}$$\end{document}H~ where η η vanishes, we can define a one-form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{w}[\eta ]$$\end{document}w[η] on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{\mathcal {H}}\setminus \mathcal {S}$$\end{document}H~\S by5.1Observe that the surface gravity of η η is given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{w}[\eta ](\eta )$$\end{document}w[η](η) on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{\mathcal {H}}\setminus \mathcal {S}$$\end{document}H~\S. Note also that, in general, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{w}[\eta ]$$\end{document}w[η] cannot be extended to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}$$\end{document}S is because the right-hand side of (5.1) vanishes, while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nabla _X\eta $$\end{document}∇Xη need not be zero. If the null hypersurface \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {H}$$\end{document}H comes from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\phi ,\xi )$$\end{document}(ϕ,ξ)-embedded null hypersurface data \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{ \mathcal {H},\gamma ,\boldsymbol{\ell }, \ell ^{(2)}, {\textbf {Y}} \}$$\end{document}{H,γ,ℓ,ℓ(2),Y} with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {U}} =0$$\end{document}U=0, the combination of (2.18) and (5.1) yields5.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \boldsymbol{w}[\nu ](X)\nu =\nabla _X\nu ={\mathop {\nabla }\limits ^{\circ }}_{X}n-{\textbf {Y}} (X,n)\nu {\mathop {=}\limits ^{\text {(2.12)}}}\big (\boldsymbol{s} -\boldsymbol{r}\big )\big (X\big )\nu \mathop {=}\limits ^{{\textbf \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\omega }$$\end{document}ω is the abstract counterpart of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{w}[\nu ]$$\end{document}w[ν]. When \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {H}$$\end{document}H admits a cross-section S, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{w}[\nu ]\vert _S$$\end{document}w[ν]|S is related to the torsion one-form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\mathfrak {s}}$$\end{document}s of S (viewed as a codimension-two spacelike submanifold), as follows:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\forall N\!\in \!\Gamma (TS)^{\perp } such that g(N,\nu )\vert _S\!\ne \!0, and \forall X\!\in \!\Gamma (TS),\\&\quad \boldsymbol{\mathfrak {s}}(X)\mathop {=}\limits ^{{\textbf {def}}}\! \!-\!\!\frac{g(\nabla _X \nu ,N)}{g(\nu ,N)}=-\boldsymbol{w}[\nu ](X). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∀N∈Γ(TS)⊥suchthatg(N,ν)|S≠0,and∀X∈Γ(TS),s(X)=def-g(∇Xν,N)g(ν,N)=-w[ν](X).For the purposes of this section, two results from the literature are particularly relevant: the near-horizon equation [3, Eq. (5.3)] of isolated horizons, and the master equation [37, Eq. (60)] of multiple Killing horizons. An isolated horizon [3] is a totally geodesic embedded null hypersurface \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {H}_{iso }$$\end{document}Hiso with product topology \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S\times \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}S×R, and which admits a null, no-where zero tangent vector field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta \in \Gamma (T\mathscr {H}_{iso })$$\end{document}η∈Γ(THiso) such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\pounds _{\eta },\mathcal {D}]=0$$\end{document}[£η,D]=0 on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {H}_{iso }$$\end{document}Hiso, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}$$\end{document}D is the connection induced from the Levi-Civita derivative of the ambient spacetime2. Under the additional assumption of η η having vanishing surface gravity on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {H}_{iso }$$\end{document}Hiso, the near-horizon equation is an identity that holds on a cross-section S of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {H}_{iso }$$\end{document}Hiso, which relates the one-form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{w}[\eta ]$$\end{document}w[η], the Ricci tensor of the induced metric h on S, and the spacetime Ricci tensor along tangential directions. On the other hand, a multiple Killing horizon \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {H}_{m }$$\end{document}Hm is a null hypersurface which is a Killing horizon with respect to at least two (linearly independent) Killing vector fields \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta _1,\eta _2$$\end{document}η1,η2 [37]. Again, when a multiple Killing horizon admits a cross-section S, the master equation is an identity on S which relates second derivatives of the proportionality function α α between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta _1$$\end{document}η1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta _2$$\end{document}η2 on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {H}_{m }$$\end{document}Hm, the one-form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{w}[\eta _1]$$\end{document}w[η1], the Ricci tensor of h, and the pull-back to S of the spacetime Ricci tensor.

While both the near-horizon and master equations have proven useful in a variety of situations (see e.g. [1, 3, 12, 13, 26, 28, 46] and references therein), they hold under very specific conditions. For isolated horizons, the hypersurface is totally geodesic, has product topology, typically satisfies energy conditions and/or the Einstein equations, and the near-horizon equation applies only on cross-sections where η η does not vanish. For multiple Killing horizons, on the other hand, one needs two Killing vectors \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta _1,\eta _2$$\end{document}η1,η2 sharing a common horizon which in addition must admit a cross-section, and even in this setting the master equation is only valid at points when both \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta _1$$\end{document}η1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta _2$$\end{document}η2 are non-zero.

At this stage, a natural question is whether one can obtain analogous equations for more general null hypersurfaces, e.g. for horizons with arbitrary topology, for null hypersurfaces containing points where η η vanishes, or for other types of horizons (e.g. homothetic or conformal Killing horizons). This is the purpose of this section. More specifically, we shall obtain a generalized form of the near-horizon equation that holds for any null hypersurface \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}$$\end{document}H equipped with a privileged tangent null vector field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\eta }$$\end{document}η¯. This equation is fully covariant and holds on the whole \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}$$\end{document}H (not only on a cross-section, which in fact need not exist). It also provides useful information about the constancy of the surface gravity of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\eta }$$\end{document}η¯.

Multiplying (5.6) by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n^a$$\end{document}na gives (5.5) since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\omega }(n)=\kappa _n$$\end{document}ω(n)=κn. □ □

Equation (5.3) is an abstract, covariant identity involving hypersurface data \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{ \mathcal {H},\gamma ,\boldsymbol{\ell }, \ell ^{(2)}, {\textbf {Y}} \}$$\end{document}{H,γ,ℓ,ℓ(2),Y}, curvature terms (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {R}_{ab}$$\end{document}Rab and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathop {R}\limits ^{\circ }}}_{ab}$$\end{document}R∘ab), derivatives of α α, the surface gravity κ κ of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\eta }$$\end{document}η¯, and the quantities \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {p}$$\end{document}p and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\Pi }^{\overline{\eta }}$$\end{document}Πη¯. Its derivation requires prescribing a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}$$\end{document}K-tuple, i.e. not only hypersurface data but also α α, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {p}$$\end{document}p, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\mathfrak {q}}$$\end{document}q. However, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {p}$$\end{document}p only appears multiplied by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {U}} $$\end{document}U, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\mathfrak {q}}$$\end{document}q only plays a role when one considers gauge transformations of the non-isolation tensor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\Pi }^{\overline{\eta }}$$\end{document}Πη¯ (recall (4.7)-(4.9)). Furthermore, the quantities \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {U}} $$\end{document}U and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\Pi }^{\overline{\eta }}$$\end{document}Πη¯ vanish for abstract Killing horizons—see the corresponding discussion in Section 4 as well as in [31, Sect. 6]—, in which case (5.3) only depends on hypersurface data plus the function α α.

As mentioned before, (5.3) generalizes in several directions the already known forms of near-horizon [3, Eq. (5.3)] and master equations [37, Eq. (60)]. First, (5.3) holds everywhere on the hypersurface and not only on a specific cross-section (in fact, we are not assuming that such a section exists). Secondly, (5.3) does not require any topological assumption on the hypersurface apart from the requirement that it is two sided (or equivalently that the radical of γ γ admits a global nowhere zero section n [36]). This is also a significant difference with respect to the works [2–4, 18, 21, 23, 37] where the hypersurface is assumed to have a product topology \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S\times \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}S×R. Regarding the vector field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\eta }$$\end{document}η¯ we have also kept full generality by allowing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\eta }$$\end{document}η¯ to vanish on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}$$\end{document}H. Observe also that, by not restricting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {U}} $$\end{document}U, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {p}$$\end{document}p and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\mathfrak {q}}$$\end{document}q in any way, we have enforced neither any specific extension of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\eta }$$\end{document}η¯ off \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi (\mathcal {H})$$\end{document}ϕ(H) (cf. Proposition 4.5), nor any particular form of the deformation tensor of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}^{\eta }$$\end{document}Kη in case the data happens to be embedded. Finally, note that (5.3) has been obtained for a fully arbitrary constraint tensor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\mathcal {R}}$$\end{document}R. We have imposed neither energy conditions nor field equations of any kind. In view of the considerations above, we call (5.3) generalized near-horizon equation. The name is justified because (5.3) holds everywhere on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}$$\end{document}H , and whenever a cross-section S exists it also yields an equation therein, i.e. the pullback of (5.3) to S.

Let us conclude this section with an immediate corollary of equations (5.4)-(5.5) regarding the constancy of the surface gravity κ κ of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\eta }$$\end{document}η¯. The function κ κ is constant along the null generators if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\Pi }^{\overline{\eta }}(n,n)=0$$\end{document}Πη¯(n,n)=0, and it is constant everywhere on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}$$\end{document}H if and only if\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Pi ^{\overline{\eta }}_{ab}n^b=\alpha \left( \mathcal {R}_{ab}n^b-(tr _P{\textbf {U}} )\omega _a+{\mathop {\nabla }\limits ^{\circ }}_a(tr _P{\textbf {U}} )\right) -P^{bc}{\mathop {\nabla }\limits ^{\circ }}_{b}\left( \alpha U _{ac}\right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Πabη¯nb=αRabnb-(trPU)ωa+∇∘a(trPU)-Pbc∇∘bαUac.Thus, for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {U}} =0$$\end{document}U=0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\Pi }^{\overline{\eta }}=0$$\end{document}Πη¯=0 the surface gravity κ κ is automatically constant along the generators, whereas it is constant everywhere on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\mathcal {R}}(n,\cdot )=0$$\end{document}R(n,·)=0 (this condition is also necessary when α α has non-empty interior).

Horizons in Lorentzian manifolds: existence results

One of the motivations for studying this problem comes from the fact that the full transverse expansion of the spacetime metric at Killing horizons with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa \ne 0$$\end{document}κ≠0 is uniquely determined by the order zero of the metric (i.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\gamma ,\boldsymbol{\ell },\ell ^{(2)}\}$$\end{document}{γ,ℓ,ℓ(2)}, cf. In this context, it is not possible to provide the entire tensor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {Y}} $$\end{document}Y from the outset, but only a posteriori can one reconstruct it from first-order transverse derivatives of the spacetime metric (cf. (2.17)). It is therefore reasonable to follow a similar procedure starting with data \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\mathcal {H},\gamma ,\boldsymbol{\ell },\ell ^{(2)},\boldsymbol{\omega },\alpha ,\boldsymbol{\Pi }^{\overline{\eta }},\boldsymbol{\mathcal {R}}\}$$\end{document}{H,γ,ℓ,ℓ(2),ω,α,Πη¯,R}, thereby extending the previous result to totally geodesic null hypersurfaces without any restrictions on the non-isolation tensor. In particular, we need to understand which properties need to be imposed on this data to guarantee that the tensor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {Y}} $$\end{document}Y defined by (6.1) agrees, after all, with the tensor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{1}{2}\phi ^{\star }(\pounds _{\xi }g)$$\end{document}12ϕ⋆(£ξg) of the constructed spacetime.

The section is organized as follows. We begin by proving a number of identities for null data with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {U}} =0$$\end{document}U=0, which will be used later (Lemmas 6.1, 6.2 and 6.3). Then, we obtain (Theorem 6.4) the necessary and sufficient conditions for the existence of a Lorentzian manifold \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {M},g)$$\end{document}(M,g) constructed from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{ \mathcal {H},\gamma ,\boldsymbol{\ell },\ell ^{(2)},\alpha ,\boldsymbol{\omega },\boldsymbol{\Pi }^{\overline{\eta }},\boldsymbol{\mathcal {R}}\}$$\end{document}{H,γ,ℓ,ℓ(2),α,ω,Πη¯,R}, with no topological restriction on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}$$\end{document}H. We continue by addressing the case when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}$$\end{document}H admits a cross-section, prescribing suitable data therein and again proving existence of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {M},g)$$\end{document}(M,g) (Theorem 6.8). The section ends with four remarks on these results (Remarks 6.10 to 6.13).

Proof

Having the identities of Lemmas 6.1, 6.2 and 6.3 are at our disposal, we can now obtain the necessary and sufficient conditions for the existence of a Lorentzian manifold where a data set of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\mathcal {H},\gamma ,\boldsymbol{\ell },\ell ^{(2)}, \boldsymbol{\omega },\alpha ,{\boldsymbol{\Pi }},{\boldsymbol{\mathcal {R}}}\}$$\end{document}{H,γ,ℓ,ℓ(2),ω,α,Π,R} is embedded. This is done in the next theorem.

Remark 6.5

As already mentioned, we are interested in allowing for the existence of zeroes of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\eta }$$\end{document}η¯ on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}$$\end{document}H. However, we do want to exclude the possibility that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\eta }$$\end{document}η¯ vanishes on open subsets of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}$$\end{document}H, since on such subsets the available information would be very limited (e.g., α α and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\Pi }^{\overline{\eta }}$$\end{document}Πη¯ would be identically zero), and some of the previous results would no longer hold. Hence, we require that the zero set of α α has empty interior on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}$$\end{document}H (cf. (iii)). This assumption is consistent with the well-known fact that Killing vectors cannot vanish on any codimension-one subset of a spacetime, unless they are identically zero.

Remark 6.6

For the quantities α α, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\omega }}$$\end{document}ω~, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Pi }}$$\end{document}Π~ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathcal {R}}}$$\end{document}R~ to satisfy 1.-4. a posteriori, one needs to prescribe the gauge-behaviours of (ii)-(iv) from the outset (recall (2.28), (4.1), (4.9) and the fact that, for embedded data, the constraint tensor is the pull-back of the ambient Ricci tensor, hence it is gauge-invariant).

Remark 6.7

The contraction of (6.20) with n yields \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Pi }}(n,n)=\alpha \widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathcal {R}}}(n,n)+n(\kappa )$$\end{document}Π~(n,n)=αR~(n,n)+n(κ). On the other hand, the contraction of (6.21) with n is (cf. (6.18)) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Pi }}(n,n)=n(\kappa )$$\end{document}Π~(n,n)=n(κ). Since the set of zeroes of α α has empty interior on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}$$\end{document}H, it follows that (6.20)-(6.21) imply \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathcal {R}}}(n,n)=0$$\end{document}R~(n,n)=0. This is consistent with the Raychaudhuri equation for totally geodesic null hypersurfaces, namely (2.21) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {U}} =0$$\end{document}U=0.

Proof

Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa \ne 0$$\end{document}κ≠0, we conclude that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\omega }}= \boldsymbol{\omega }\mathop {=}\limits ^{{\textbf {def}}}\boldsymbol{s}-{\textbf {Y}} (n,\cdot )=\boldsymbol{s}-\frac{1}{2}\phi ^{\star }(\pounds _{\xi }g)$$\end{document}ω~=ω=defs-Y(n,·)=s-12ϕ⋆(£ξg), hence item 2. holds. Now, the data \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\mathcal {H},\gamma ,\boldsymbol{\ell },\ell ^{(2)},\mathbb {Y}^{(1)}={\textbf {Y}} \}$$\end{document}{H,γ,ℓ,ℓ(2),Y(1)=Y} fulfills Lemma 6.3 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Pi }}$$\end{document}Π~, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathcal {R}}}$$\end{document}R~ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\omega }=\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\omega }}$$\end{document}ω=ω~, so in particular (6.13) holds. This, together with (6.22), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa \ne 0$$\end{document}κ≠0 and the fact that the zero-set of α α has empty interior ensures that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi ^{\star }\big ({\textbf {Ric}} _g\big )=\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathcal {R}}}$$\end{document}ϕ⋆(Ricg)=R~, which proves 4.. Item 3. then follows from combining (6.23), (6.26), (6.27) and (6.28). This concludes the proof of (6.20)-(6.22) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \, \Longrightarrow \, $$\end{document}⟹ 1.-4..

It only remains to show that the pair \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\phi ,\xi )$$\end{document}(ϕ,ξ) satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{1}{2}\phi ^{\star }(\pounds _{\xi }g)={\textbf {Y}} $$\end{document}12ϕ⋆(£ξg)=Y both under conditions 1.-4. and (6.20)-(6.22). We have already shown that 1.-4. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \, \Longrightarrow \, \frac{1}{2}\phi ^{\star }(\pounds _{\xi }g)={\textbf {Y}} $$\end{document}⟹12ϕ⋆(£ξg)=Y, so it only remains to prove that (6.20)-(6.22) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \, \Longrightarrow \, \frac{1}{2}\phi ^{\star }(\pounds _{\xi }g)={\textbf {Y}} $$\end{document}⟹12ϕ⋆(£ξg)=Y. Suppose that, from (6.20)-(6.22), one constructs any Lorentzian manifold \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {M},g)$$\end{document}(M,g) satisfying 1.-4. but with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{1}{2}\phi ^{\star }(\pounds _{\xi }g)={\textbf {Y}} '\ne {\textbf {Y}} $$\end{document}12ϕ⋆(£ξg)=Y′≠Y. Then 1.-4. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \, \Longrightarrow \, {\textbf {Y}} =\frac{1}{2}\phi ^{\star }(\pounds _{\xi }g)={\textbf {Y}} '\ne {\textbf {Y}} $$\end{document}⟹Y=12ϕ⋆(£ξg)=Y′≠Y, which is a contradiction. □ □

Null hypersurfaces that admit a foliation by cross-sections are particularly interesting in General Relativity. For instance, in the standard characteristic initial value problem one prescribes data on two null hypersurfaces that intersect transversely on a spacelike surface, which forces them to admit cross-sections. In the following theorem, we address the case when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}$$\end{document}H has product topology \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S\times \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}S×R, S being a cross-section. We show that, given totally geodesic null metric data, together with suitable data on S plus two scalar functions and a symmetric (0, 2)-tensor on the hypersurface, the existence of a Lorentzian manifold realizing the prescribed quantities is guaranteed.

Remark 6.9

Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\alpha },\boldsymbol{\upvarpi },\mathcal {T}$$\end{document}α¯,ϖ,T are only defined on S, their gauge behaviours are only imposed therein.

We conclude the paper with some comments on Theorems 6.4 and 6.8.

Remark 6.10

The only restriction imposed on the tensor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathcal {R}}}$$\end{document}R~ prescribed in Theorems 6.4 and 6.8 is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathcal {R}}}(n,n)=0$$\end{document}R~(n,n)=0. This, as mentioned in Remark 6.7, is necessary for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathcal {R}}}$$\end{document}R~ to be consistent with the Raychaudhuri equation (2.21) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {U}} =0$$\end{document}U=0. Except for this, no other condition is imposed, such as e.g. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathcal {R}}}$$\end{document}R~ being zero o proportional to γ γ. Theorems 6.4 and 6.8 are hence independent of any field equations.

The problem of existence of a Λ Λ-vacuum spacetime where a given abstract Killing horizon data can be embedded was analyzed in [42, Thm. 5.8], where it was found that it is sufficient to complement (a)-(b) with the condition that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\tau }(n)$$\end{document}τ(n) is constant and non-zero. All in all, the results in this section can be viewed as a generalization from the Killing horizon case in Λ Λ-vacuum to the case of arbitrary vector fields \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\eta }$$\end{document}η¯ with no restrictions on the field equations. For such general setting we have demonstrated that (6.20) and (6.21) are not sufficient by themselves and they must be supplemented with (6.22) in order to guarantee the existence of a spacetime where the data can be embedded.

Remark 6.13
